The [Bell X-22A][ref_x22] had four propellers, each inside a tilting duct, two forward and two aft. It first flew on 17 March 1966, one of the two aircraft was destroyed five months later, and the survivor went on flying into the 1980s, until October 1984 in the summary of the [Wikipedia entry][ref_x22] and until 1988 in the account of the [Vertical Flight Society][ref_vertipedia_x22]. **Even the earlier date makes a research career of about eighteen years**, twice the nine years and four months over which the X-15 flew. This article is the twenty-third in the [X-Planes series][related_post_a297_xplanes_framing], following the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], the [X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], the [X-3][related_post_a300_douglas_x3], the [X-4][related_post_a301_northrop_x4], the [X-5][related_post_a302_bell_x5], the [X-6][related_post_a303_convair_x6], the [X-7][related_post_a304_lockheed_x7], the [X-8][related_post_a305_aerojet_x8], the [X-9][related_post_a306_bell_x9], the [X-10][related_post_a307_north_american_x10], the [X-11][related_post_a308_convair_x11], the [X-12][related_post_a309_convair_x12], the [X-13][related_post_a310_ryan_x13], the [X-14][related_post_a311_bell_x14], the [X-15][related_post_a312_north_american_x15], the [X-16][related_post_a313_bell_x16], the [X-17][related_post_a314_lockheed_x17], the [X-18][related_post_a315_hiller_x18], the [X-19][related_post_a316_curtiss_wright_x19], the [X-20][related_post_a317_boeing_x20], and the [X-21][related_post_a318_northrop_x21].

**It did not spend those years being an X-22.** From 1968 it carried a variable-stability system that let it imitate the handling of other aircraft in flight, and what it produced was not data about four-ducted-propeller aeroplanes but data about how any vertical-takeoff aircraft ought to handle. **The most successful vertical-takeoff research aircraft of the period succeeded by ceasing to be a prototype and becoming an instrument.**

The standard inventory entry is [Jenkins Landis and Miller 2003 American X-Vehicles, An Inventory X-1 to X-50][book_jenkins_landis_miller_2003] and the vehicle compilation is [Miller 2001 The X-Planes, X-1 to X-45][book_miller_2001]. The programme's own retrospective is [The Bell X-22A V/STOL, Variable Stability Research Airplane, Lessons Learned][research_lessons_1990].

## The Research Question

The series has now met four ways of getting a propeller-driven aeroplane off the ground vertically. The [X-13][related_post_a310_ryan_x13] stood on its tail. The [X-14][related_post_a311_bell_x14] deflected jet thrust. The [X-18][related_post_a315_hiller_x18] tilted its whole wing and the [X-19][related_post_a316_curtiss_wright_x19] tilted four propellers on outriggers. **The X-22 tilts four propellers as well, but it puts each one inside a duct**, and almost everything that distinguishes this aeroplane follows from that decision.

A shroud around a propeller is not a fairing. It changes the momentum balance, it carries part of the thrust on its own inner surface, and in forward flight it behaves like a short annular wing. Its period theory is in [Kruger 1949][research_kruger_1949], [Lerbs 1953][research_lerbs_1953], [Thwaites 1953][research_thwaites_1953], [Van Niekerk 1958][research_van_niekerk_1958], [Theodorsen 1960][research_theodorsen_1960_2], [Ordway et al 1960][research_ordway_1960], [Morgan 1961][research_morgan_1961], and [Greenberg and Ordway 1964][research_greenberg_ordway_1964], its static tests begin with [Platt 1948][research_platt_1948], and the large-scale tests of the period are summarised in [Mort 1966][research_kenneth_w_mort_1966]. The whole literature is gathered in the cluster on ducted propellers, shrouds and lift fans below.

**The question this article asks is what the duct is worth and what it costs**, because the answer turns out to be large in both directions and the two effects arrive at different points in the flight.

**The period record behind that question is very large and it is where this article's weight sits.** Between the war and the end of the 1960s the vertical-takeoff problem was worked over by every laboratory that had a wind tunnel, across ducted and open propellers, tilting wings, deflected slipstreams and jet lift. Examples are the ducted-propeller assault transport studies in [Zabinsky 1956][research_zabinsky_1956], the static thrust of open propellers in [Coward 1955][research_coward_1955], tilt-wing design in [McCormick and Mallen 1957][research_mccormick_mallen_1957], the deflected slipstream in [Kuhn and Grunwald 1960][research_kuhn_grunwald_1960], jet lift in [Smith 1961][research_smith_1961], and the state of the art as the NASA conferences and the national survey of the period recorded it in [NASA Conference on V/STOL 1960][research_nasa_conference_1960], and [Campbell 1964][research_john_p_campbell_1964]. The period records are spread across the clusters below, with the largest shares in the clusters on ducted propellers and on vertical and short takeoff configurations. Most of those configurations were tried once and abandoned, which is why the literature is broad and shallow, and it is the body of work every aircraft in this part of the series was designed on top of.

## Programme Origin

The X-22A was a tri-service programme, ordered by the Navy on behalf of itself, the Army and the Air Force, to explore whether a ducted-propeller vertical-takeoff transport was practical. Bell had already flown the [X-14][related_post_a311_bell_x14] and had a long involvement in vertical flight, and the four-duct tandem layout had been under investigation in wind tunnels for several years before the contract, in [Larry A. Young][research_larry_a_young], [Ronald C Busan et al][research_ronald_c_busan], [Siena K S Whiteside and Beau P Pollard][research_siena_k_s_whiteside_beau_p_pollard], [Goodson and Yaggy 1960][research_goodson_yaggy_1960], [Tapscott and Kelley 1960][research_tapscott_kelley_1960], [Kelley 1962][research_kelley_1962], [McKinney and Newsom 1962][research_mc_kinney_newsom_1962], [Clark et al 1963][research_clark_1963], [Newsom 1963][research_newsom_1963], [Curtiss 1965][research_curtiss_h_c_1965], [Biggers et al 1966][research_biggers_1966], [Freeman and Newsom 1966][research_freeman_newsom_1966], [Xiang et al 2016][research_xiang_2016], [Geuther et al 2020][research_steven_c_geuther_2020], [Zhang et al 2020][research_zhang_2020_2], [Lee et al 2021][research_lee_2021], [Meng et al 2026][research_meng_2026].

**The X-22A was the Navy's part of the Tri-Service Assault Transport Program**, in the account of the [Vertical Flight Society][ref_vertipedia_x22], and its layout was chosen by arithmetic about engines. A NASA design study of a modern tiltduct, [Whiteside and Pollard][research_siena_k_s_whiteside_beau_p_pollard], reports from the Bell design study that cross-shafted arrangements of two, three, four and five ducts were considered, and that four were chosen on engine weight and availability with the thrust-to-weight ratio needed for an engine-out hover in view. The variable-stability system was designed into the aircraft before it flew, as [Beilman's 1965 description of the X-22A variable stability system][ref_beilman_1965] shows.

**Two aircraft were built.** The first was rolled out on 25 May 1965 and made its first hovering flight in March 1966, according to the Vertical Flight Society. It was written off in August of the same year after a hard landing, which the [Wikipedia entry][ref_x22] attributes to the failure of a propeller control and the Vertical Flight Society to a hydraulic failure after about three hours of flying. The two accounts are compatible if the hydraulic failure was in the propeller pitch actuation, but no primary account of the accident is available. **The crew survived and the second aircraft absorbed the lesson**, which is a better outcome than the [X-18][related_post_a315_hiller_x18] managed with the same class of failure.

## The Aircraft

Four ducts of 7 feet diameter, each containing a three-bladed propeller. The rear wing spans 39 feet 3 inches and the forward one 22 feet 11 inches over the ducts, the aircraft is 39 feet 7 inches long, and it weighs 10,478 pounds empty against a gross weight of 15,287 and a maximum takeoff weight of 17,644, in the specification of the [Wikipedia entry][ref_x22].

**The duct itself is documented at full scale.** A full-scale duplicate of the X-22A ducted propeller was tested in the Ames 40 by 80 foot wind tunnel, in [Gamse and Mort 1967][research_gamse_mort_1967], and their table of dimensions gives a duct chord of 49 inches, a propeller tip clearance of 0.4 inch, a net area of 37.84 square feet at the propeller, a net exit area of 44.08 square feet and an exit vane of 31.8 square feet.

Four General Electric YT58-GE-8D turboshaft engines of 1,250 shaft horsepower each drive all four propellers through a cross-shafted transmission, so that any engine can drive any duct. The 1,250 horsepower is also the design power per duct at which Mort and Gamse evaluated the duct's thrust.

$$A_{d} = \frac{\pi D^{2}}{4} = \frac{\pi \times 7^{2}}{4} = 38.48\ \text{ft}^{2}$$

Four of those give 153.9 square feet of disc, and at 14,600 pounds, the three-engine hover weight assumed here, which no primary document read for this article states,

$$\frac{W}{A} = \frac{14{,}600}{153.9} = 94.8\ \text{lbf/ft}^{2}$$

**That is a very high disc loading.** A helicopter of the period sits between five and ten. The [X-18][related_post_a315_hiller_x18] and [X-19][related_post_a316_curtiss_wright_x19] used large propellers and sat far below it. The X-22 is closer to a lift jet than to a rotorcraft, and the consequences run through everything that follows.

## Sizing From First Principles

### What the Shroud Buys

Take a propeller inside a duct whose exit area is $\sigma$ times the disc area. Continuity and momentum across the disc give the thrust and the ideal power in terms of the exit velocity,

$$T = \rho \sigma A V_{e}^{2} \qquad P = \tfrac{1}{2} \rho \sigma A V_{e}^{3}$$

Eliminating $V_e$,

$$P = \frac{1}{2} \frac{T^{3/2}}{\sqrt{\rho \sigma A}} \qquad \Rightarrow \qquad T \propto \sigma^{1/3}$$

**An open propeller is the case $\sigma = 1/2$**, because its wake contracts to half the disc area far downstream, and substituting that recovers the familiar $T^{3/2}/\sqrt{2\rho A}$. So a duct that merely prevents the contraction, with $\sigma = 1$, gives

$$\frac{T_{\text{duct}}}{T_{\text{open}}} = (2\sigma)^{1/3} = 2^{1/3} = 1.26$$

**Twenty-six percent more static thrust for the same power, from a ring of metal that does no work.** A mildly diffusing duct does better still. That is the whole reason for the configuration, and it is what makes a seven-foot propeller viable at ninety-five pounds per square foot.

The X-22A duct is one of those. Its measured area ratio is

$$\sigma_{d} = \frac{A_{e}}{A_{p}} = \frac{44.08}{37.84} = 1.165$$

with $A_e$ the net exit area and $A_p$ the net area at the propeller, from the table in [Gamse and Mort 1967][research_gamse_mort_1967], and the same ideal relation then gives

$$\frac{T_{\text{duct}}}{T_{\text{open}}} = (2\sigma_{d})^{1/3} = 2.330^{1/3} = 1.33$$

The hover arithmetic below keeps $\sigma = 1$ and lets the figure of merit absorb the difference.

**The tunnel measured the real thing.** Mort and Gamse report a maximum figure of merit of 81 percent based on the net exit area and a maximum propulsive efficiency of 74 percent. For free-stream speeds up to about 100 knots the measured thrust at the design 1,250 horsepower follows ideal ducted momentum theory with a propeller efficiency of 90 percent. Above that speed it falls away from the ideal curve, and they attribute the shortfall to the drag of the duct, centre body and struts, most of which is not skin friction.

The ideal power to hover 14,600 pounds on 153.9 square feet is 3,750 horsepower for an open rotor and

$$P_{\text{ideal}} = \tfrac{1}{2} \frac{W^{3/2}}{\sqrt{\rho A}} = 2{,}651\ \text{hp}$$

for the ducted case, which is $1/\sqrt{2}$ of it.

### What the Quoted Margin Means

The programme is described by the [Vertical Flight Society][ref_vertipedia_x22] as having 35 percent excess power so that it could hover with one engine out. The NASA survey of in-flight simulators, [NASA TM-81156][ref_nasa_tm81156], states the same number as a vertical thrust-to-weight ratio of 1.35 that allows a single-engine-out hover. **The two readings give very different margins, and the arithmetic shows how different.**

Four engines give 5,000 shaft horsepower and three give 3,750. If the installed power exceeds what hovering requires by 35 percent, then what remains after losing an engine is

$$\frac{3}{4} \times 1.35 = 1.0125$$

**One and a quarter percent.** On the power reading the number is the smallest that makes a three-engine hover arithmetically possible, with nothing to spare.

On the thrust reading, thrust at a fixed figure of merit scales as power to the two-thirds, so losing a quarter of the power leaves

$$\frac{T_{3}}{W} = 1.35 \left( \frac{3}{4} \right)^{2/3} = 1.35 \times 0.8255 = 1.114$$

**an eleven percent thrust margin with an engine out**, which is what the NASA report's sentence describes. The NASA wording is the primary statement, so the thrust reading is the documented one, and the power reading that follows is a test of the secondary figure rather than a description of the aircraft.

The power reading is still checkable, because it fixes the figure of merit. If hovering at 14,600 pounds requires 5,000 divided by 1.35, then

$$\text{FM} = \frac{P_{\text{ideal}}}{P_{\text{required}}} = \frac{2{,}651}{3{,}704} = 0.716$$

**That is a thoroughly ordinary figure of merit for a ducted lift fan**, which is the first check. The second is stronger. Feeding 0.716 back through momentum theory gives the weight that three engines can actually lift,

$$W_{3} = W \left( \frac{P_{3} \, \text{FM}}{P_{\text{ideal}}} \right)^{2/3} = 14{,}721\ \text{lbf}$$

against the 14,600 assumed here. **Agreement to 0.83 percent shows that the 35 percent and the 14,600 pounds are consistent with each other on the power reading**, but because no primary document read here states the 14,600 pounds, the agreement checks two secondary figures against each other and not against the aircraft.

**The full-scale tunnel figure separates the readings only partly.** A figure of merit referred to the net exit area converts to the disc-area basis used here through the ideal power, which varies as the inverse square root of the area,

$$\text{FM}_{\text{disc}} = \text{FM}_{\text{exit}} \sqrt{\frac{A_{e}}{A_{d}}} = 0.81 \sqrt{\frac{44.08}{38.48}} = 0.867$$

On the power reading the installed figure of merit of 0.716 is 83 percent of the tunnel's best, and the difference would have to be accounted for by transmission losses, download on the wing and fuselage, and duct interference. On the thrust reading, the tunnel's best figure of merit and the rated 5,000 horsepower give a four-engine thrust of

$$T_{4} = \left( 2\,\text{FM}_{\text{disc}}\, P \sqrt{\rho A} \right)^{2/3} = 20{,}261\ \text{lbf}$$

and a thrust-to-weight ratio of 1.35 at 15,008 pounds, essentially the 15,000 pound design gross weight that [Gamse and Mort 1967][research_gamse_mort_1967] use to set their trim conditions. Three engines would then hover 16,725 pounds. **That agreement assumes no installation loss at all**, so it is an upper bound, and the record read here does not settle which reading the programme intended. What it does settle is that the NASA survey gives the 35 percent as thrust.

On the power reading, with the figure of merit of 0.716, the same arithmetic is unkind about the rest of the weight range.

| condition | weight, lbf | power required, shp | four engines | three engines |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| three-engine vertical takeoff | 14,600 | 3,704 | +35.0% | +1.3% |
| gross | 15,287 | 3,968 | +26.0% | −5.5% |
| maximum takeoff | 17,644 | 4,920 | +1.6% | −23.8% |

**On that reading, at maximum takeoff weight the X-22 could not hover on three engines and had 1.6 percent in hand on four.** Vertical takeoff was available at the bottom of the weight range and nowhere near the top, which is the recurring fact about every vertical-takeoff aircraft in this series and is stated here in numbers rather than in adjectives.

### The Downwash That Buys

High disc loading is not free at the bottom of the aircraft either. The exit velocity follows from the thrust per duct,

$$V_{e} = \sqrt{\frac{T}{\rho \sigma A_{d}}} = \sqrt{\frac{3{,}650}{0.0023769 \times 38.48}} = 200\ \text{ft/s}$$

**One hundred and thirty-six miles per hour, straight down, from four ducts at once.**

Disc loading is the parameter that sets all of this, and the relations are short. The induced velocity and the power loading follow from it alone,

$$v_{e} = \sqrt{\frac{W/A}{\rho}} \qquad \frac{T}{P} = \frac{2\,\text{FM}}{v_{e}}$$

so that lifting a pound costs power in proportion to the square root of the disc loading.

| $W/A$, lbf/ft² | $v_e$, ft/s | lbf per horsepower |
|---|---|---|
| 5 | 45.9 | 17.2 |
| 10 | 64.9 | 12.1 |
| 40 | 129.7 | 6.1 |
| 94.8 | 199.7 | 3.9 |

**A helicopter of the period sat between five and ten and lifted about three and a half times as much per horsepower.** The measurement and prediction of hover performance across that range is in [Dyke 1963][research_dyke_1963], [Shivers 1967][research_shivers_1967], [Kocurek and Tangler 1977][research_kocurek_tangler_1977], [Felker 1993][research_felker_1993], [Ramasamy et al 2010][research_ramasamy_2010], [Overmeyer and Martin 2017][research_overmeyer_martin_2017], and [Jimenez Garcia and Barakos 2018][research_jimenez_garcia_barakos_2018], gathered with the rest in the cluster on hover performance below, and it is the literature in which the penalty for choosing a small, heavily loaded disc is quantified rather than asserted. That is the standing charge for choosing ducts, and it is paid in every second of hover. The dynamic pressure in that jet is 47 pounds per square foot. The ground erosion, the recirculation and the hazard to anyone nearby are all consequences of the same choice that made the duct worth having, and they are treated in [Morse and Newhouse 1960][research_morse_newhouse_1960], [Obryan 1960][research_obryan_1960], [Morse and Newhouse 1961][research_morse_newhouse_1961_2], [O'Bryan 1961][research_o_bryan_1961], [George et al 1964][research_george_1964], [Kuhn 1965][research_kuhn_1965], [Pruyn and Taylor 1971][research_pruyn_taylor_1971_2], and [Ison 2024][research_ison_2024_2], and in the cluster on downwash, ground effect and recirculation below.

## What the Shroud Charges

A duct held at an angle to the oncoming air captures a mass flow and turns it onto the duct axis. Turning momentum requires a force, and the reaction on the duct is a normal force,

$$N_{\text{mom}} = \dot{m} V_{\infty} \sin\alpha \qquad \dot{m} = \rho A_{d} \left( V_{\infty} \cos\alpha + v_{i} \right)$$

The rounded lip adds a suction force of the same order, so the total is roughly twice the momentum term. **The duct is behaving as an annular wing**, and the theory and measurement of that are [Goodson and Grunwald 1962][research_goodson_grunwald_1962], [Mort 1965][research_mort_1965], [Curtiss and Howard C. 1969][research_curtiss_howard_c_1969], [Mann 1974][research_mann_1974], [Walker 1981][research_walker_1981], [Lee and Kwok 1983][research_lee_kwok_1983], [Gill and D’Andrea 2020][research_gill_dandrea_2020].

**This has to be evaluated along a conversion, not on a grid.** A duct angle of forty-five degrees at two hundred feet per second is not a flight condition, and tabulating it produces a normal force larger than the aeroplane.

### The Corridor Can Be Derived, Not Assumed

The wing area is not published, which looks like a dead end and is not one. At the end of a conversion the ducts are horizontal and the wings carry the whole weight, so the conversion speed fixes the area,

$$S = \frac{2W}{\rho V_{\text{conv}}^{{2}} C_{L,\max}} = \frac{2 \times 14{,}600}{0.0023769 \times 203^{{2}} \times 1.4} = 213\ \text{ft}^{{2}}$$

**That number has to survive a geometric check and it does.** Two thirds of it on the 39.25 foot rear span is a mean chord of 3.62 feet, and the remaining third on the 22.92 foot forward span is 3.10 feet. Chords of three to four feet on a forty-foot aeroplane are what the photographs show.

With an area the corridor follows from the force balance. Writing $i$ for the duct angle above the horizontal,

$$T \sin i + L = W \qquad T \cos i = D$$

$$\tan i = \frac{W - L}{D} \qquad T = \sqrt{(W-L)^{{2}} + D^{{2}}}$$

with $L = q S C_L$ and $D = q (S C_{D_w} + f)$, where $f$ is the equivalent flat-plate area of the four ducts computed below and the wing drag coefficient $C_{D_w}$ is the 0.10 assumed here. The trim of a vertical-takeoff aircraft through its conversion is a subject with a period literature of its own, in [Seibel 1963][research_seibel_1963], [Englar and Kirkpatrick 1969][research_englar_kirkpatrick_1969], [Cheng and Pei 2023][research_cheng_pei_2023], [Lee et al 2026][research_lee_2026], and the quantity it turns on is how much lift the wing is developing at each speed, which for a propeller aircraft is not a wing-alone question. **The slipstream changes the lift over whatever part of the wing it washes**, and that effect has been measured since the 1930s, in [Stuper 1938][research_stuper_1938], [Franke and Weinig 1939][research_franke_weinig_1939], [Brenckmann 1958][research_brenckmann_1958], [Kuhn and Grunwald 1960][research_kuhn_grunwald_1960], [Winston and Huston 1962][research_matthew_m_winston_robert_j_huston_1962], [Goland et al 1964][research_goland_1964], [Butler et al 1966][research_butler_1966], and [Rizk 1980][research_rizk_1980], and in the cluster on propeller slipstream and wing interaction below.

**Thrust required falls monotonically through the conversion**, because the wing takes the lift faster than the drag builds, so the corridor was never thrust-limited. Whatever made the X-22 hard to convert, it was not power.

| $V$, ft/s | knots | duct angle | $\dot m$, slug/s | $N$, lbf | $N/T$ |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 20 | 11.8 | 81.1 | 18.6 | 733 | 0.20 |
| 60 | 35.5 | 63.4 | 20.7 | 2,224 | 0.61 |
| 100 | 59.2 | 45.7 | 24.7 | 3,528 | 0.97 |
| 120 | 71.1 | 36.8 | 27.1 | 3,890 | 1.07 |
| 140 | 82.9 | 27.9 | 29.6 | 3,881 | 1.06 |
| 180 | 106.6 | 10.2 | 34.5 | 2,197 | 0.60 |
| 200 | 118.5 | 1.3 | 36.6 | 340 | 0.09 |

**The normal force peaks at about its own duct's thrust, and it peaks in the middle of the conversion.** The table samples the conversion at seven speeds and the true maximum lies between two of its rows, at 130 feet per second, where the normal force reaches 3,941 pounds against a duct thrust of 3,650, a ratio of 1.08.

**The linear schedule is close to what was flown.** The Calspan account of the third research programme, [Lebacqz and Aiken 1975][research_lebacqz_aiken_1975], states that for the X-22A the centre of the allowable transition corridor is well approximated by a linear relationship between duct angle and flight velocity, and notes that tilt-duct aircraft have a fairly narrow corridor bounded by buzz and buffet.

### The Corridor With the Wing at Full Lift

The linear schedule is not the only one a force balance permits. Evaluating the same normal force along the **derived** corridor, where the wing pulls its full lift coefficient at every speed and the ducts make up the difference, gives something much worse. The ducts stay at large angles far later than a linear ramp suggests, and the momentum estimate climbs to 8,266 pounds per duct at 180 feet per second, or **2.26 times the whole aircraft weight over four ducts**.

**That is not a credible force and saying so is the point.** More than twice the weight, applied sideways in mid-conversion, would be unflyable and unmissable. So the model is wrong there, and the useful question is where it stops being right.

The momentum-turning argument assumes the duct captures its own streamtube and turns all of it. That holds while the crossflow component is small compared with the velocity the duct itself induces, so the validity parameter is

$$\mu = \frac{V_{\infty} \sin i}{v_{i}}$$

| $V$, ft/s | knots | duct angle | $\mu$ | $N/T$ | status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 40 | 23.7 | 89.6 | 0.20 | 0.40 | model holds |
| 60 | 35.5 | 89.2 | 0.30 | 0.60 | model holds |
| 100 | 59.2 | 87.2 | 0.50 | 1.02 | marginal |
| 140 | 82.9 | 82.1 | 0.69 | 1.52 | model invalid |
| 180 | 106.6 | 60.7 | 0.79 | 2.26 | model invalid |
| 200 | 118.5 | 11.2 | 0.20 | 0.77 | model holds |

**The parameter passes 0.5 at 100 feet per second and comes back below it at 195.** The momentum model is therefore valid at both ends of the conversion and invalid in the middle, **and the middle is exactly where a tilt aircraft is hard to fly.**

What caps the force in that band is not momentum. It is that the lip cannot hold the flow round it at that incidence and separates, which is the known weakness of every ducted fan and which now has a computed boundary rather than a mention. **The honest statement is that the normal force reaches about the duct's own thrust where the model can be trusted, and that beyond it the binding limit is lip stall.** Over four ducts that is of the order of the aircraft weight, applied perpendicular to the duct axes, at exactly the speed where the wings are not yet working and the ducts are no longer pointing at the ground.

**The measured record places lip separation in descent rather than in level conversion.** On the full-scale duct, [Gamse and Mort 1967][research_gamse_mort_1967] found from pressure distributions that separation over the whole upstream lip, which is what changes forces and power, occurs on a representative four-duct configuration only at low power and high duct angle, as in descending flight, while partial separation in level flight brings no large change in force. The large-scale tandem model of [Biggers et al 1966][research_biggers_1966] showed duct stall only on the front ducts and limited the estimated descent rate of a 6,500 pound aeroplane of that layout to about 600 feet a minute at a duct incidence near 60 degrees. In flight, the initial Navy tests reported by Rhodes in 1969 and summarised in [NASA CR-1901][ref_wolkovitch_1971] identified lip separation as the cause of duct buzz, a high-frequency vibration through the whole aircraft, and suggested a descent-angle limit of about 10 degrees at low speed. At 40 knots, or 67.5 feet per second, a 10 degree path is a sink rate of

$$\dot{h} = 60\, V \sin\gamma = 60 \times 67.5 \times \sin 10^{\circ} = 703\ \text{ft/min}$$

which is the same order as the tunnel estimate.

**The measured pitching moment peaks earlier than the momentum normal force.** Biggers and his colleagues found the untrimmed pitching moment largest at about 60 degrees of duct incidence, and [Whiteside and Pollard][research_siena_k_s_whiteside_beau_p_pollard] summarise the earlier Ames semispan tests as placing the pitch-up maximum near 45 knots at a duct angle near 60 degrees. On the linear schedule, with the ducts vertical at rest and horizontal at 203 feet per second,

$$i = 90^{\circ} \left( 1 - \frac{V}{V_{\text{conv}}} \right) \qquad V_{60^{\circ}} = \frac{203}{3} = 67.7\ \text{ft/s} = 40\ \text{kt}$$

against 130 feet per second, or 77 knots, where the momentum estimate of the normal force peaks. **The moment the controls must trim arrives in the first third of the conversion**, and the momentum-turning model, which places the whole force at one point on the lip, does not locate it.

**That is the charge for the twenty-six percent.** The shroud that makes the hover affordable is an annular wing that has to be flown through the conversion, and the aeroplane which needs the shroud most in hover is the one that has the most trouble in between.

### The Ducts Are Also the Drag

The maximum speed in the specification of the [Wikipedia entry][ref_x22], 278 knots at 10,000 feet, provides a check on all of this from a direction the hover calculation does not touch, although the record disagrees about whether it was flown. The [Vertical Flight Society][ref_vertipedia_x22] gives a maximum of 315 miles an hour, or 274 knots, Wikipedia's own text says that the required 283 knots was never reached, and [NASA TM-81156][ref_nasa_tm81156] gives 150 knots as the maximum speed of the aircraft in its research configuration. Taking 278 knots, an installed 5,000 shaft horsepower and the propulsive efficiency of 0.80 assumed here,

$$D = \frac{\eta P}{V} = \frac{0.80 \times 5{,}000 \times 550}{469.2} = 4{,}689\ \text{lbf}$$

$$f = \frac{D}{q} = \frac{4{,}689}{193.3} = 24.3\ \text{ft}^{{2}}$$

**A clean aeroplane of this weight would have five to eight square feet.** The X-22 has 24.3, and the lift-to-drag ratio at that condition is 3.26.

Now the same number from geometry. Four ducts of seven-foot diameter present 153.9 square feet of frontal area, so the equivalent flat plate implies a duct drag coefficient of

$$C_{D,\text{duct}} = \frac{f}{4 A_{d}} = \frac{24.3}{153.9} = 0.158$$

which is an ordinary value for a short annular body with a propeller inside it. **The maximum speed and the duct geometry agree**, and they were not fitted to one another. Estimating the drag of external bodies on an aeroplane in this way is old, standardised work, in [Bonner et al 1975][research_bonner_1975], [Neal 1975][research_neal_1975], [Smetana 1975][research_smetana_1975], [Corsiglia et al 1979][research_corsiglia_1979], [Corsiglia et al 1981][research_corsiglia_1981], [Malone and Peavey 1999][research_malone_peavey_1999].

**The measured propulsive efficiency is lower than the one assumed.** [Gamse and Mort 1967][research_gamse_mort_1967] report a maximum of 74 percent for the full-scale duct, and with that value the same relations give

$$D = \frac{0.74 \times 5{,}000 \times 550}{469.2} = 4{,}337\ \text{lbf} \qquad f = \frac{4{,}337}{193.3} = 22.4\ \text{ft}^{2} \qquad C_{D,\text{duct}} = \frac{22.4}{153.9} = 0.146$$

so the duct drag coefficient lies between 0.146 and 0.158 on the two efficiencies. Their tunnel result also says where the drag comes from. The drag measured with the propeller removed was much larger than the estimated skin friction of the shroud, and they conclude that a duct designed for high speed could have had a significantly higher propulsive efficiency.

**That is the third face of the same trade.** The shroud buys twenty-six percent of thrust in the hover, charges an annular wing's normal force in the conversion, and then charges again in cruise by presenting four times thirty-eight square feet of frontal area to the airstream for the rest of the flight.

## Dependent Systems

### The Elevons Are in the Jet

In hover there is no free-stream dynamic pressure at all, so a conventional control surface has nothing to work with. The duct exits have plenty.

$$q_{\text{jet}} = \tfrac{1}{2} \rho V_{e}^{2} = 47.4\ \text{lbf/ft}^{2}$$

The duct normal force acts near the lip rather than at the duct centre, because the lip is where the flow is turned, so each duct contributes a nose-up couple of about

$$M = \frac{N \ell}{2}$$

for a duct of length $\ell$. At the largest defensible normal force and the four-foot duct, whose chord is 49 inches in the table of Mort and Gamse, that is 7,900 foot-pounds per duct and **31,500 foot-pounds over four**, and the tandem layout does not cancel it, because both duct pairs tilt the same way and both couples act nose-up about their own centres.

Something has to trim that, and the X-22A uses two effectors whose roles exchange through the conversion. The ducts carry spanwise elevons across their exits, immersed in the propeller slipstream, and the propellers have collective and differential blade pitch. In the hover, according to [NASA TM-81156][ref_nasa_tm81156] and [Freeman and Newsom 1966][research_freeman_newsom_1966], pitch and roll come from differential blade pitch between front and rear and between left and right, and yaw comes from differential elevon deflection, because a vane in a vertical jet turns the jet sideways. A mechanical mixer proportions the pilot's commands between propellers and elevons as a function of duct angle, and in forward flight pitch comes from differential deflection of the front and rear elevons. In the large-scale tunnel model of [Biggers et al 1966][research_biggers_1966], longitudinal trim came from differential fore and aft exit-vane deflection between cruise and 40 degrees of duct incidence and from differential fore and aft thrust above 50 degrees.

**The elevons are large.** The exit vane of the full-scale duct is 31.8 square feet, and the elevons of the 0.18-scale model of Freeman and Newsom scale up by the square of the length ratio to

$$S_{e} = 0.82 \times 5.56^{2} = 25.3\ \text{ft}^{2} \qquad S_{e} = 1.06 \times 5.56^{2} = 32.8\ \text{ft}^{2}$$

for the front and rear ducts. At the lift coefficient of 0.9 assumed here, a vane of 31.8 square feet in the hover jet gives

$$F = q_{\text{jet}} S_{e} C_{L_{e}} = 47.4 \times 31.8 \times 0.9 = 1{,}357\ \text{lbf}$$

which is **more than a third of the duct's own thrust**, a ratio of 0.37, turned sideways. That is why one vane per duct is enough for yaw in the hover.

**The free stream does not reach that dynamic pressure until about 200 feet per second**, which is the end of the conversion, so through the conversion the elevons work in the propeller slipstream rather than in the free stream. The design provided control power beyond trim in a 35 knot wind of 0.60 radian per second squared in pitch, 1.00 in roll and 0.56 in yaw, in Freeman and Newsom, whose free-flight model pilots needed about 110, 80 and 30 percent of the scaled values for the manoeuvres they flew. By 1979 the variable-stability aircraft had nominal hover control powers of 3.20, 3.45 and 0.75 radians per second squared in pitch, roll and yaw, according to NASA TM-81156.

### The Cross-Shaft

Four engines drive four propellers through a common transmission, treated in [Leishman 1966][research_leishman_1966], [Overfield and Crawford 1967][research_overfield_crawford_1967], [Envia 2016][research_envia_2016], [Casadei et al 2019][research_casadei_2019], [Yan and Chen 2019][research_yan_chen_2019], [Yu et al 2020][research_yu_2020], [Leelaburanathanakul et al 2021][research_leelaburanathanakul_2021], [Hoogreef and Soikkeli 2022][research_hoogreef_soikkeli_2022], [Kang et al 2023][research_kang_2023], [Zhang et al 2024][research_zhang_2024_2], [Aroussi 2025][research_aroussi_2025], [Hung and Dai 2025][research_hung_dai_2025], [Nabadova and Sabziev 2025][research_nabadova_sabziev_2025], [Jokar and Khoshnood 2026][research_jokar_khoshnood_2026], [Mokotoff and Cinar 2026][research_mokotoff_cinar_2026].

**Cross-shafting adds no power.** What it changes is the failure mode. Without it, losing an engine loses a duct, and losing one of four ducts on a machine with a rear semi-span of 19.6 feet applies

$$M = T_{\text{duct}} \, y = 3{,}650 \times 19.62 = 71{,}600\ \text{ft}\cdot\text{lbf}$$

of rolling moment that has to be trimmed out by the remaining three. With cross-shafting the same failure removes a quarter of the power and none of the symmetry.

The shaft itself is undramatic in torque. At the 700 foot per second tip speed assumed here the propellers turn at about 1,910 revolutions per minute, inside the range of 1,200 to 2,590 at which Mort and Gamse ran the full-scale duct, so each absorbs

$$Q = \frac{P}{\omega} = \frac{1{,}250 \times 550}{200} = 3{,}440\ \text{ft}\cdot\text{lbf}$$

and with one engine out the three survivors deliver 938 horsepower to each of four propellers, so **312 horsepower has to cross the airframe**, a quarter of one engine.

**It was not undramatic in heat.** By 1979 the engine and fan systems were limited operationally to hover at air temperatures below 80 degrees Fahrenheit by gearbox temperature limits, which [NASA TM-81156][ref_nasa_tm81156] notes precluded significant amounts of hover in the summer, and allowance for an engine-out hover limited operation further according to atmospheric conditions.

Sizing a drive train of this kind is rotorcraft engineering rather than aeroplane engineering, and it has its own literature, in [Doblhoff 1956][research_doblhoff_1956], [Ward 1970][research_ward_1970], [Cormier 1979][research_cormier_1979], [Dean 1979][research_dean_1979], [Mancini 1983][research_mancini_1983], [White 1983][research_white_1983], [Albrecht 1988][research_albrecht_1988], [Tokarski et al 1994][research_tokarski_1994].

**The cost of cross-shafting is not the shaft.** It is that every gearbox must accept power from either direction, and that the whole train becomes a single path whose failure takes everything. The first aircraft was lost to the failure of a part that the test pilot Stanley Kakol, in Wikipedia's account, called the only non-redundant component in the power chain, which is the same lesson from the other side.

**This is the X-18's fatal deficiency, answered.** That aircraft had two engines with no interconnection, and the accident that ended it was the asymmetry rather than the shortfall. The X-22 was designed so that the asymmetry cannot happen, and the price is a transmission that has to carry full power to any duct from any engine.

## The Flight Test Record

First flight 17 March 1966. **The first aircraft was lost on 8 August 1966** in a hard landing, the date given by the [Wikipedia entry][ref_x22], after about three hours of flying in which it had been tested to transition angles of up to 30 degrees and speeds of up to 100 knots, according to the [Vertical Flight Society][ref_vertipedia_x22]. Both crew survived and the aircraft was written off.

The second aircraft flew on, performing hundreds of complete transitions and, on 30 July 1968, a hover above 8,000 feet that the Vertical Flight Society records as a record. Its variable-stability and control system, designed by the Cornell Aeronautical Laboratory, later Calspan, was demonstrated in early 1968, and from that point its purpose changed. **It stopped being a prototype of a configuration and became a flying simulator of configurations that did not exist**, in [Harper and Robert P. 1955][research_harper_robert_p_1955], [Creer and McNeill 1956][research_creer_mc_neill_1956], [Marchese 1963][research_marchese_1963], [Newell et al 1963][research_newell_1963], [Garren and Kelly 1965][research_garren_kelly_1965], [Person and Robbins 1965][research_person_robbins_1965], [Determination of flight characteristics 1967][research_determination_of_1967], [Rhoads 1967][research_rhoads_1967], [Szalai 1967][research_szalai_1967], [Eney 1968][research_eney_1968], [Klopfstein 1968][research_klopfstein_1968], [Malcom 1969][research_malcom_1969], [Bell Aerospace Co Buffalo Ny 1970][research_bell_aerospace_co_buffalo_ny_1970], [Rhoads 1970][research_rhoads_1970], [Schuler et al 1972][research_schuler_1972], [Beilman 1973][research_beilman_1973], [Smith et al 1973][research_smith_1973], [Lebacqz 1974][research_lebacqz_1974], [Smith 1974][research_smith_1974], [Lebacqz and Aiken 1975][research_lebacqz_aiken_1975], [Motyka 1975][research_motyka_1975], [Aiken et al 1977][research_aiken_1977], [Corliss et al 1977][research_corliss_1977], [Parker 1986][research_parker_1986], [Henschel and Chetty 1989][research_henschel_chetty_1989], [Sato and Satoh 2011][research_sato_satoh_2011], [Sato 2020][research_sato_2020], [Scholten et al 2020][research_scholten_2020], [Chandrasekaran et al 2021][research_chandrasekaran_2021], [Network Solution for Real-time Synchronization in Flight Simulator 2022][research_network_solution_2022], [Yahui et al 2024][research_yahui_2024], [Yi and Feng 2025][research_yi_feng_2025].

### How the Variable-Stability System Worked

**The system was a response-feedback design**, the most common mechanization of the period, in which sensed motion variables command control deflections in proportion, so that changing the gains changes the closed-loop aircraft. [NASA TM-81156][ref_nasa_tm81156] describes it in detail. It is a four-axis system, in thrust, pitch, roll and yaw, primarily analogue with an on-board digital computer, with electrohydraulic servos driving the right-hand flight controls through the same linkages the right-hand pilot moves in normal flight. The evaluation pilot sits on the left, mechanically disconnected from the right-hand controls, and flies through artificial-feel servos whose force gradients run from 1.5 pounds per inch to a stiff stick in pitch and roll. The safety pilot on the right sets up each configuration in flight. Feedback gains are programmable with airspeed from 30 knots rearward to 150 knots forward, using an omnidirectional airspeed system with a second sensor on the nose boom for the vertical component in hover. A limited-authority feedforward system acts as a vernier on blade pitch and elevon angle, because a closed-loop variable-stability aircraft needs its actuators placed very precisely. The ducts rotate together between 0 and 95 degrees at no more than 5 degrees a second.

**A response-feedback aircraft does not know exactly what it is simulating.** The resulting dynamics are not known in advance, so each configuration has to be identified from its own flight records, which is the subject of [Lebacqz 1974][research_lebacqz_1974]. The Calspan team used a Kalman filter technique to estimate the derivatives the system had produced, among them the angle-of-attack stability and pitch rate damping varied in the first programme and every roll and yaw derivative varied in the second.

**The limits are stated as plainly as the capabilities.** The variable-stability system could not be used above about 125 knots, which excluded the upper end of the conversion. It had only limited uncoupled direct force in the longitudinal and vertical directions and none sideways. Hover was restricted by gearbox temperature as described above. And the airframe had an established service life of 500 flight hours, of which 80 were expected to remain at the end of 1980, so the aircraft would by then have flown about

$$500 - 80 = 420\ \text{h}$$

against the about 200 hours the Vertical Flight Society gives for its whole career. The two cannot both be right, and the NASA figure is the primary one.

### The Research Programmes

NASA TM-81156 summarises the programmes flown through 1979, and the Calspan papers report them in detail. The first three were sponsored by an interagency steering group of the Navy, the Air Force, NASA Langley and the Federal Aviation Administration, and the later ones by the Navy alone.

**The first programme measured short-term longitudinal dynamics for the steep approach of a short takeoff and landing, or STOL, aircraft.** Two pilots made 50 evaluations of 18 combinations of short-period frequency and damping at 65 and 80 knots, on approach paths of 6 to 10 degrees, visual and on instruments, reported in [Schuler et al 1972][research_schuler_1972] and [Smith et al 1973][research_smith_1973]. Lebacqz's account gives sixteen combinations at a 9 degree glide slope, and the two counts are not reconciled in the documents read. Essentially no data of this kind existed for STOL approaches before it, and supplying them for the V/STOL flying-qualities specification, MIL-F-83300, was a main purpose. It showed that steep instrument approaches could be flown satisfactorily with only instrument landing system information, given good short-term dynamics, and the results were applied in the development of the YC-14 and YC-15.

**The second measured lateral-directional dynamics and roll control power.** Three pilots flew 109 evaluations of 17 sets of dynamics at a 7.5 degree glide slope and 65 knots, varying the roll mode time constant, Dutch roll frequency, roll due to sideslip and yaw due to aileron, with roll authority limited electrically for some sets. Two findings bear directly on the specification. Satisfactory flying qualities needed about twice the roll damping that MIL-F-83300 specified, and about half its roll control power. Since the roll mode time constant is the inverse of the roll damping derivative,

$$\tau_{R} = -\frac{1}{L_{p}}$$

doubling the damping halves the time constant. The same programme expanded the system into the hover and made the first vertical landings ever performed on a variable-stability system.

**The third flew instrument transitions to the hover**, in [Lebacqz and Aiken 1975][research_lebacqz_aiken_1975]. The task was a hooded approach from 100 knots, capturing a 7.5 degree glide slope and decelerating at about 0.05 g to a hover at 100 feet. Decelerating uniformly from 100 knots, or 168.8 feet per second, at that rate takes

$$s = \frac{V^{2}}{2a} = \frac{168.8^{2}}{2 \times 0.05 \times 32.17} = 8{,}854\ \text{ft} \qquad t = \frac{V}{a} = 105\ \text{s}$$

which is consistent with the deceleration beginning about 8,000 feet out in still air, as the paper gives it, and continuing through the flare. On the glide slope at 100 knots the aircraft descends at

$$\dot{h} = 60\, V \sin\gamma = 60 \times 168.8 \times \sin 7.5^{\circ} = 1{,}322\ \text{ft/min}$$

Thirty-seven evaluations covered five display formats, from position information alone to four-axis control directors, against five levels of control augmentation, in a flight experiment of 45 hours. **The central result is a trade between control and display**, in which more augmentation needs less display and the reverse. Translational velocity had to be displayed for any acceptable system, and because the basic X-22A has low natural height damping near the hover, a thrust director was needed whenever the pilot also had to manage the duct angle. The programme also devised a duct-angle command, the Independent Thrust Vector Inclination Command, which follows the linear corridor described earlier.

**The fourth simulated the AV-8B** for a decelerating instrument approach. Forty-three evaluations of 22 configurations, drawn from six flight control schemes and seven head-up display formats, showed that no display gave consistently satisfactory ratings with the proposed rate stability augmentation, while rate-command and attitude-command systems did with several displays, and the resulting display formats were being considered for the AV-8B. **The fifth**, begun in April 1978 and planned through 1980, addressed hover and low-speed criteria for visual and instrument flight and then transition criteria.

The four completed programmes add up to

$$50 + 109 + 37 + 43 = 239$$

piloted evaluations in NASA TM-81156, each a configuration flown and rated.

The final flights are dated October 1984 in the summary of the Wikipedia entry and 1988 in its text and in the Vertical Flight Society's account. No primary document read here records the end of flying.

## Comparison With Ground Prediction

The four-duct tandem layout had been in wind tunnels since the late 1950s, and the aerodynamic characteristics measured there are in [Larry A. Young][research_larry_a_young], [Ronald C Busan et al][research_ronald_c_busan], [Siena K S Whiteside and Beau P Pollard][research_siena_k_s_whiteside_beau_p_pollard], [Goodson and Yaggy 1960][research_goodson_yaggy_1960], [Tapscott and Kelley 1960][research_tapscott_kelley_1960], [Kelley 1962][research_kelley_1962], [McKinney and Newsom 1962][research_mc_kinney_newsom_1962], [Clark et al 1963][research_clark_1963], [Newsom 1963][research_newsom_1963], [Curtiss 1965][research_curtiss_h_c_1965], [Biggers et al 1966][research_biggers_1966], [Freeman and Newsom 1966][research_freeman_newsom_1966], [Xiang et al 2016][research_xiang_2016], [Geuther et al 2020][research_steven_c_geuther_2020], [Zhang et al 2020][research_zhang_2020_2], [Lee et al 2021][research_lee_2021], [Meng et al 2026][research_meng_2026]. On the gross behaviour the tunnels were right. The thrust augmentation, the general shape of the conversion, and the pitching behaviour were all anticipated.

**The full-scale duct test anticipated the duct's performance well.** [Gamse and Mort 1967][research_gamse_mort_1967] measured a figure of merit and a propulsive efficiency that bracket the figures this article derives, found the thrust following ideal ducted theory to about 100 knots, and located the upstream lip separation that flight later reported as duct buzz in descent.

**The free-flight model anticipated the handling problems.** The 0.18-scale model of [Freeman and Newsom 1966][research_freeman_newsom_1966] had unstable control-fixed pitching and rolling oscillations in hover, with periods of 3.4 and 3.0 seconds that scale with the square root of the length ratio,

$$T_{\text{full}} = T_{\text{model}} \sqrt{5.56} \qquad 3.4 \times 2.358 = 8.0\ \text{s} \qquad 3.0 \times 2.358 = 7.1\ \text{s}$$

slow enough that the pilots found them easy to control. Near the ground the fan slipstreams met between the fore and aft ducts and formed an upward flow that went erratically into one fan or the other, the model gained 20 percent in lift from an upload on the fuselage, and the disturbances were severe enough that the authors concluded artificial stabilisation would be needed in ground effect. Through most of the conversion the model had an unstable Dutch roll and very low directional stability, and stall of the upper outside duct surfaces caused wing dropping on the approach, although the authors note that small ducts stall differently from full-scale ones.

**Where the ground work was weakest is where it usually is, which is the interference between parts.** The rear ducts operate in air the forward ducts have already worked on, and the wake of a duct is not the wake of an open propeller. Downwash testing of the tandem ducted configuration was done specifically because that interference could not be predicted, and it remains the part of the problem a model shows least well. The large-scale model of [Biggers et al 1966][research_biggers_1966] measured some of it. The static thrust of the complete model matched that of four isolated ducts, so interference in the hover was negligible, but in the conversion duct stall appeared only on the front ducts, because their downwash delayed stall on the rear ones, and the interference lowered the configuration's stall boundary below that of an isolated duct.

**Flight added a characteristic the tunnels did not isolate.** The Calspan pilots met a low natural height damping near the hover in the basic aircraft, with vertical damping that fell as the duct angle increased, and the guidance in [Lebacqz and Aiken 1975][research_lebacqz_aiken_1975] compensated with a gain that rose with duct angle.

## What the Data Changed

**The X-22 settled that the configuration flies.** Nothing in eighteen years of operation suggested that a four-duct tandem vertical-takeoff aircraft was uncontrollable or unsafe, which is a stronger negative result than the [X-18][related_post_a315_hiller_x18] or the [X-19][related_post_a316_curtiss_wright_x19] produced.

**What it did not settle is whether anyone wanted one.** The arithmetic of the useful load is short and unkind,

$$\frac{W_{\text{payload}}}{W} = \frac{1{,}200}{14{,}600} = 0.082$$

against an empty weight of 10,478 pounds and a useful load of 4,122, most of which is fuel. The 1,200 pound maximum payload is from the specification in the [Wikipedia entry][ref_x22], and the [Vertical Flight Society][ref_vertipedia_x22] gives the same figure, with six passengers as the alternative. **Weight estimation is the discipline that decides whether a configuration survives contact with a requirement**, and it is a methodical subject rather than a guess, in [Torenbeek 1972][research_torenbeek_1972], [Ascani 1974][research_ascani_1974], [Hayase 1974][research_hayase_1974], [Hayase 1974][research_hayase_1974_2], [Hayase 1974][research_hayase_1974_3], [Hayase 1974][research_hayase_1974_4], [Hayase 1974][research_hayase_1974_5], [Hayase 1974][research_hayase_1974_6], [Hiyama 1974][research_hiyama_1974], [Martindale et al 1974][research_martindale_1974], [Wildermuth et al 1974][research_wildermuth_1974], [Wildermuth et al 1974][research_wildermuth_1974_2], [DeBilzan 1975][research_debilzan_1975], [Onat and Tolle 1979][research_onat_tolle_1979], [Hajela and Chen 1988][research_hajela_chen_1988]. The disc loading that the duct makes affordable also produces a 136 mile per hour downwash, a severe weight sensitivity in the hover, and a payload of 1,200 pounds on an aircraft of 15,000. The configuration works and the mission arithmetic is unattractive, which is a different verdict from the one the earlier tilt aircraft received.

**The largest thing it changed is not about ducts at all.** The variable-stability system turned the aircraft into a source of handling-qualities data for vertical-takeoff aircraft generally. That literature runs from the proposed V/STOL criteria of [Curry et al 1965][research_curry_1965], and [Anderson 1966][research_anderson_1966] through the specification of [Key 1971][research_key_1971] and the simulator work that supported its further development in [Vinje and Miller 1973][research_vinje_miller_1973] to the later Navy criteria of [Clark 1982][research_clark_1982] and the retrospectives of [Ashkenas 1984][research_ashkenas_1984], and [Harper and Cooper 1986][research_harper_cooper_1986], and it is gathered in the cluster on handling and flying qualities criteria below, with the aircraft's own programme reports, such as [Smith 1974][research_smith_1974], and [Aiken et al 1977][research_aiken_1977], in the first cluster. The X-22 is cited far more often for what it measured about flying than for anything it demonstrated about ducts.

**The specification work is where that is most direct.** MIL-F-83300, the military specification for the flying qualities of piloted V/STOL aircraft, was generated around 1970, in [Key 1971][research_key_1971], and the X-22A's first programme was flown in large part to supply it with STOL approach data that did not otherwise exist. Its second programme then found the specification's roll damping requirement too low by about a factor of two and its roll control power requirement about twice what satisfactory flying qualities needed, according to [NASA TM-81156][ref_nasa_tm81156]. Those are corrections in both directions, measured in flight, and they are what a variable-stability aircraft is for.

Ducted fans are in the middle of the largest revival they have ever had, which the cluster on ducted propellers, shrouds and lift fans below counts, in work such as [Ohanian et al 2012][research_ohanian_2012], [Deng et al 2020][research_deng_2020], [Zhang and Barakos 2021][research_zhang_barakos_2021], [Jin et al 2018][research_jin_2018], [Akturk and Camci 2022][research_akturk_camci_2022], and [Liu et al 2025][research_liu_2025]. The broader vertical-takeoff field around them, from the control of multirotor vehicles to urban air mobility, is gathered in the clusters on multirotor and unmanned vehicles and on eVTOL aircraft, and is surveyed in [Saied et al 2024][research_saied_2024], [Xu et al 2025][research_xu_2025], and [Darvishpoor et al 2025][research_darvishpoor_2025].

**Almost nothing in that revival changes the aerodynamics.** The momentum theory is the same, the thrust augmentation is the same, and the three charges the shroud levies are the same three this article computes. What changed is the machinery, and the machinery changed everything about how the configuration is used.

### Electric Propulsion Dissolved the Cross-Shaft

The X-22's most expensive single decision was the cross-shafted transmission, and it was forced. Four turboshafts driving four propellers cannot be allowed to fail independently, because losing one duct applies 71,600 foot-pounds of rolling moment, so every gearbox had to accept power from either direction and the whole train became one failure path.

**An electric machine removes the requirement rather than solving it**, in [Distributed Electric Propulsion 2018][research_distributed_electric_2018], [Weng et al 2021][research_weng_2021], [Li et al 2022][research_li_2022], [Darmstad et al 2024][research_darmstad_2024], [Siddavatam et al 2024][research_siddavatam_2024], [Chen et al 2025][research_chen_2025], [Xiao et al 2025][research_xiao_2025], and [Ma et al 2025][research_ma_2025], and in the cluster on electric, hybrid-electric and distributed propulsion below. Motors can be sized with margin individually, they can be fed from separate buses, and a failure removes one rotor's thrust without any mechanical coupling to the others. The redundancy moves from the shaft to the wiring.

**And it changes how thrust is controlled.** Every propeller on a shafted system turns at the same speed, so the X-22 changed one duct's thrust by changing its blade pitch, and that differential blade pitch was its primary pitch and roll control in the hover, with the elevons in its duct exits providing yaw. An electric rotor can instead change thrust by changing speed, and a fixed-pitch rotor controlled that way needs no variable-pitch hub. **The control principle is the X-22's, and the mechanism that delivered it is what has gone.**

### The Lip Still Stalls

The specific weakness of a ducted fan is that the lip cannot hold the flow round it at large incidence or in a crosswind, and it separates, in [Jacocks and Kneile 1975][research_jacocks_kneile_1975], [Mugridge 1975][research_mugridge_1975], [Groeneweg 1977][research_groeneweg_1977], [Seidel and Matwey 1983][research_seidel_matwey_1983], [Motycka 1985][research_motycka_1985], [Sedlock 1985][research_sedlock_1985], [J P Longley and E M Greitzer 1992][research_j_p_longley_e_m_greitzer_1992], [Xu et al 2015][research_xu_2015], [Minaker and Defoe 2019][research_minaker_defoe_2019], [Minaker and Defoe 2019][research_minaker_defoe_2019_2], [Akturk and Camci 2022][research_akturk_camci_2022], [Chennuru et al 2025][research_chennuru_2025].

**This article computes where the momentum model stops describing the flow, and the X-22A record shows where the lip actually let go**, in descent at low power and high duct angle, with a buzz felt through the whole aircraft. Nothing about that has moved. A modern ducted electric vertical takeoff and landing aircraft, or eVTOL, sitting in a crosswind on a rooftop is in exactly the condition that limited the X-22, and the literature treats it as a current design problem rather than a solved one.

### The Normal Force in Transition Is Still the Hard Part

The duct as an annular wing at incidence is [Goodson and Grunwald 1962][research_goodson_grunwald_1962], [Mort 1965][research_mort_1965], [Milla and Blick 1966][research_milla_blick_1966], [Curtiss and Howard C. 1969][research_curtiss_howard_c_1969], [Mann 1974][research_mann_1974], [Walker 1981][research_walker_1981], [Lee and Kwok 1983][research_lee_kwok_1983], and [Gill and D’Andrea 2020][research_gill_dandrea_2020], and the conversion it complicates is [Nardizzi et al 1971][research_nardizzi_1971], [Huntley 1972][research_huntley_1972], [Hill 1983][research_hill_1983], [Jiang and Zhang 2021][research_jiang_zhang_2021], [Pfeifle and Fichter 2023][research_pfeifle_fichter_2023], [Li et al 2020][research_li_2020], and [Zhou et al 2025][research_zhou_2025], with the rest in the cluster on transition and conversion below.

**What has changed is the tool rather than the answer.** The article's momentum estimate with a lip factor is what could be done with a slide rule, and it fails in the middle of the conversion for reasons the article states. The modern equivalent computes the flow, and it finds the same thing, which is a large moment appearing in mid-conversion that the control system has to be sized for. **The X-22 met that with elevons in its jets at low duct angles and differential blade pitch at high ones. A modern aircraft meets it with rotor speed, which is why the transition control problem is now posed as an allocation problem** rather than as a question about where to put a surface.

### Hover Efficiency Has Not Improved and Cannot

The disc-loading relations in this article are momentum theory, so they are not going to be superseded, and the modern hover measurements are [Helf et al 1973][research_helf_1973], [Velkoff 1981][research_velkoff_1981], [Lee and Dassonville 2020][research_lee_dassonville_2020], [Son and Kim 2023][research_son_kim_2023].

**A ducted lift fan at ninety-five pounds per square foot pays about three and a half times what a helicopter pays per pound lifted, and it always will.** Electric propulsion changes the source of the power and not the amount of it. That is the single hardest number in the whole field and it is the reason every proposed vertical-takeoff transport keeps arriving at a small payload fraction.

### Downwash Became a Public Problem

The 136 mile per hour downwash this article computes was, in 1966, a matter for the ground crew. It is now a matter for the safety standards of the places such aircraft land, in [Ison 2024][research_ison_2024_2], resting on measurements of downwash and outwash such as [Chaderjian et al 2002][research_chaderjian_2002], [Ramasamy and Yamauchi 2017][research_ramasamy_yamauchi_2017], [Tan et al 2018][research_tan_2018], [Mehrabi and Davari 2020][research_mehrabi_davari_2020], and [Wu et al 2026][research_wu_2026], and on the rest of the cluster on downwash, ground effect and recirculation below.

**High disc loading near people is the constraint that the X-22 never had to answer to**, because it flew from military airfields. An aircraft expected to operate from a city rooftop has to account for what its jets do to anyone standing nearby, and the arithmetic that produces the jet velocity is the same arithmetic that produces the hover efficiency. **The two cannot be traded against each other. They are the same number.**

### Noise Became a Requirement

Ducted fan acoustics, gathered in the cluster on ducted fan, rotor and vertical-takeoff noise below, run from the period analysis of ducted turbomachinery noise in [Zorumski and Lester 1974][research_zorumski_lester_1974] to the shape of the duct inlet in [Shur et al 2018][research_shur_2018], acoustic liners in [Azimi et al 2014][research_azimi_2014], and [Sato et al 2024][research_sato_2024], ducted lift fans designed for low noise in [Zhang et al 2024][research_zhang_2024_3], and [Zhao et al 2025][research_zhao_2025], and the community noise of urban air mobility in [Rizzi and Rafaelof 2021][research_rizzi_rafaelof_2021], and [Raza and Stansbury 2025][research_raza_stansbury_2025].

The duct is genuinely helpful here, because it shields the blades and gives somewhere to put a liner, and that is a real reason to choose the configuration that did not weigh in 1962. **It is also the clearest case in this survey of a modern advantage that the X-22 could not have collected**, since nobody was choosing a military research aircraft's configuration for its noise.

### Certification Is Where the Field Now Lives

The X-22 spent eighteen years measuring how vertical-takeoff aircraft ought to handle, and the modern version of that question is certification, in [Cardoso et al 2022][research_cardoso_2022], [Lim et al 2023][research_lim_2023], [Norsetter and Smith 2024][research_norsetter_smith_2024], [Pleffken 2026][research_pleffken_2026], and [Shubert et al 2026][research_shubert_2026], resting on handling-qualities work for eVTOL and multicopter aircraft such as [Bahr et al 2021][research_bahr_2021], [Li et al 2024][research_li_2024_4], [Favaro et al 2025][research_favaro_2025], [Wang et al 2025][research_wang_2025_2], and [Jusko and Berger 2026][research_jusko_berger_2026], and on the wider literature in the cluster on handling and flying qualities criteria below.

**This is the aircraft's actual legacy and it is worth being plain about it.** The X-22 is cited far more often for handling-qualities data than for anything about ducts. The criteria that a modern vertical-takeoff aircraft must satisfy to be certified rest on a body of in-flight simulation work of which the X-22 was, for two decades, the principal instrument.

### In-Flight Simulation Outlived the Configuration

Variable-stability aircraft are [Harper and Robert P. 1955][research_harper_robert_p_1955], [Creer and McNeill 1956][research_creer_mc_neill_1956], [Drinkwater and Rolls 1962][research_drinkwater_rolls_1962], [Marchese 1963][research_marchese_1963], [Newell et al 1963][research_newell_1963], [Garren and Kelly 1965][research_garren_kelly_1965], [Person and Robbins 1965][research_person_robbins_1965], [Determination of flight characteristics 1967][research_determination_of_1967], [Rhoads 1967][research_rhoads_1967], [Szalai 1967][research_szalai_1967], [Eney 1968][research_eney_1968], [Klopfstein 1968][research_klopfstein_1968], [Malcom 1969][research_malcom_1969], [Michaels 1969][research_michaels_1969], [Bell Aerospace Co Buffalo Ny 1970][research_bell_aerospace_co_buffalo_ny_1970], [Rhoads 1970][research_rhoads_1970], [Schuler et al 1972][research_schuler_1972], [Beilman 1973][research_beilman_1973], [Smith et al 1973][research_smith_1973], [Lebacqz 1974][research_lebacqz_1974], [Smith 1974][research_smith_1974], [Lebacqz and Aiken 1975][research_lebacqz_aiken_1975], [Motyka 1975][research_motyka_1975], [Aiken et al 1977][research_aiken_1977], [Corliss et al 1977][research_corliss_1977], [Corliss et al 1978][research_corliss_1978], [Parker 1986][research_parker_1986], [Henschel and Chetty 1989][research_henschel_chetty_1989], [Sato and Satoh 2011][research_sato_satoh_2011], [Ko and Park 2014][research_ko_park_2014], [Sato 2020][research_sato_2020], [Scholten et al 2020][research_scholten_2020], [Chandrasekaran et al 2021][research_chandrasekaran_2021], [Fang et al 2022][research_fang_2022], [Network Solution for Real-time Synchronization in Flight Simulator 2022][research_network_solution_2022], [Yahui et al 2024][research_yahui_2024], [Yi and Feng 2025][research_yi_feng_2025].

**The technique the X-22 was converted to use is now routine and is mostly done differently.** Ground simulators improved, and where an airborne simulator is still wanted it is usually a helicopter or a small jet rather than a purpose-built vertical-takeoff machine. **The X-22 was the last of a line as well as the best of it.**

### What the Survey Shows

**The duct's arithmetic was correct in 1962 and is correct now.** Twenty-six percent more static thrust for a ring of metal, an annular wing's normal force through the conversion, frontal area in cruise, and a hover bill set by disc loading. None of that has moved, and none of it is in dispute.

**What came back is not the X-22's aeroplane but the X-22's component**, attached to a machine that has no shafts, changes thrust by changing speed, and is being asked to fly near people. The three charges are unchanged and two of the answers are new.

**And the thing the X-22 was actually for turned out to be the thing that lasted.** The configuration was not adopted. The handling-qualities data was, and the criteria a modern ducted eVTOL will be certified against descend from eighteen years of an aeroplane pretending to be other aeroplanes over Niagara Falls.

### The X-22A and the ducted-propeller assault transport

Reports and papers whose titles name the X-22A, together with the 1956 studies of a ducted-propeller assault transport from which the four-duct configuration descends.

**Of the 16 titles that name the X-22A, 11 name flying qualities, variable stability, simulation, identification or displays, and none names the duct.** They run from 1963 to 1990. The aircraft's ducted propulsion is documented in tunnel reports whose titles do not name it, of which the full-scale duct test of Mort and Gamse is the most important, and that is the shape the Source Base describes.

### The duct at incidence and the lip

The ducted propeller or fan at angle of attack, in non-axial inflow and in crosswind, and separation at the duct lip.

**The subject the conversion depends on is small and recent.** Of the 18 records, 9 date from 2015 or later and two from 1965 or earlier, the shrouded-propeller tunnel tests of the NACA and NASA that measured the loads on an isolated duct through the whole range of incidence.

### Handling and flying qualities criteria

Handling and flying qualities criteria and specifications for V/STOL aircraft, helicopters, rotorcraft and eVTOL aircraft, with the conventional-aircraft criteria and pilot rating methods they rest on.

**The cluster is weighted to the X-22's working life.** Of the 204 records, 99 date from 1966 to 1989, and six titles name MIL-F-83300.
