## Where the Framing Breaks Down

The thrust augmentation figure assumes an ideal duct with no internal losses and a specific exit area ratio which is assumed rather than measured. A real duct has friction, tip clearance and a diffuser that may separate, and the figure of merit derived here absorbs all of it into a single number.

**The normal force calculation is the weakest part of the article.** It is momentum turning multiplied by an assumed lip factor of two, and the sensitivity is large, giving a peak of anywhere between 0.54 and 1.35 times the aircraft weight over four ducts as that factor runs from one to two and a half. With $k$ the lip factor and $N_{\text{mom}}$ the momentum term at the peak, half of the 3,941 pounds computed with $k = 2$,

$$\frac{4 N_{\max}}{W} = \frac{4 k N_{\text{mom}}}{W} = \frac{4 k \times 1{,}971}{14{,}600} = 0.54\, k$$

**The magnitude is not robust, and neither is the location**, since the measured pitching moment peaks near 60 degrees of duct incidence, well before the momentum estimate's peak.

**The conversion corridor is derived rather than assumed**, from a wing area that the conversion speed implies rather than one taken from a data sheet. That derivation rests on an assumed maximum lift coefficient of 1.4 and on the assumption that the wings carry the whole weight at the conversion speed, and the resulting area is checked against the two spans rather than against a published figure.

**The weakness of the normal force is documented rather than hidden.** The momentum model overestimates it badly wherever the crossflow parameter exceeds about one half, which is most of the middle of the conversion, and the article marks the number the model produces there as not credible rather than relying on it.

The tandem interference enters only through the tunnel and model results reported above, which show it protecting the rear ducts from stall and unsettling the hover near the ground. No relation in the article models it.

**Several figures are assumed rather than sourced**, and each is labelled where it is used. They are the 14,600 pound three-engine hover weight, the propulsive efficiency of 0.80 at maximum speed, the wing drag coefficient of 0.10 in the corridor calculation, the maximum lift coefficient of 1.4, the lip factor of two, the 700 foot per second tip speed and the elevon lift coefficient of 0.9.

## The Source Base

The X-22 is well documented on the flying-qualities side and documented on the ducts mainly by tunnel tests, of which the full-scale test of its own duct is the most important. The programme's own retrospective is [The Bell X-22A V/STOL, Variable Stability Research Airplane, Lessons Learned][research_lessons_1990], written twenty-four years after first flight, and the tri-service description of the aircraft is contemporary with the contract.

**The primary documents behind the account of the vehicle are NASA reports.** [NASA TM-81156][ref_nasa_tm81156] describes the aircraft, its variable-stability system and its research programmes through 1979. [Gamse and Mort 1967][research_gamse_mort_1967] report the full-scale duct, [Biggers et al 1966][research_biggers_1966] and [Freeman and Newsom 1966][research_freeman_newsom_1966] the large-scale and free-flight models, [Lebacqz 1974][research_lebacqz_1974] and [Lebacqz and Aiken 1975][research_lebacqz_aiken_1975] two of the Calspan programmes, and [NASA CR-1901][ref_wolkovitch_1971] the initial military flight tests at second hand. The programme's retrospective of 1990 and the 1969 report of the initial military tests are not freely available, the NASA records of [Aiken et al 1977][research_aiken_1977] and [Smith 1974][research_smith_1974] carry no full text, and the Defense Technical Information Center reports cited here, among them [Bell Aerospace Co Buffalo Ny 1970][research_bell_aerospace_co_buffalo_ny_1970], were not retrievable. The accident, the first flight of the second aircraft, the last flight and the maximum speed therefore rest on secondary accounts that disagree with each other.

**The imbalance is a consequence of what the aircraft became.** Eighteen years of variable-stability work generated a large literature about handling qualities in which the X-22 is the instrument rather than the subject, and comparatively little was published about the ducted propulsion after the configuration stopped being a candidate for production.

**The research survey holds 1,630 research records drawn from the NASA Technical Reports Server, the Defense Technical Information Center and the journal literature indexed by Crossref**, of which 480 are report primaries, being 29.4 percent, by the identifier rule set out under The Definition of Primary. **The median publication year is 2012.** Of the 1,598 dated records, 256 were published in 1965 or earlier, before the aircraft flew, being 16.0 percent, and 772 were published in 2015 or later, being 48.3 percent.

### The Definition of Primary

**Primacy is a property of the document, and this survey derives it from the identifier.** A record is a report primary when its address is on the NASA Technical Reports Server or it carries a digital object identifier under the 10.21236 prefix, which Crossref registers for Defense Technical Information Center reports. **The vehicle's own record is primary under that rule only in part.** Of the 16 titles that name the X-22A, 10 are report primaries. The papers presented to the American Helicopter Society and to the American Institute of Aeronautics and Astronautics, among them the programme's 1990 retrospective, and a 1973 journal description of the aircraft as an in-flight simulator are not, so 29.4 percent is a lower bound on how much of the survey is first-hand. The NASA survey of in-flight simulators and the NASA contractor report on terminal guidance, on which much of the account of the aircraft rests, are curated references rather than survey records.

### Thin Subjects

**Seven subjects that this article's derivations depend on have thin literatures.** Each count below is the number of research records in this survey whose title carries at least one of the listed terms, with a hyphen read as a space. Where a row joins two groups of terms with the word together, a title must carry a term from each group.

| Subject | Title terms counted | Records |
|---|---|---|
| the normal force and pitching moment of a duct at incidence | normal force, pitching moment together with duct, ducted, shroud, shrouded, fan, propfan | 1 |
| thrust augmentation by a shroud | thrust augmentation, augmentation ratio, static thrust together with duct, ducted, shroud, shrouded | 3 |
| the cross-shafted transmission | cross shaft, cross shafting, cross shafted, interconnect, interconnected, shaft coupled | 1 |
| duct buzz | buzz | 0 |
| height damping and height control in the hover | height damping, vertical damping, height control, heave damping, vertical rate command | 4 |
| vanes and elevons in a duct exit | vane, vanes, elevon, elevons, deflector together with duct, ducted, shroud, shrouded | 2 |
| hover with an engine out | engine out, engine failure, engine inoperative, one engine together with hover, VTOL, V/STOL, vertical, tilt rotor, eVTOL, helicopter | 7 |

**The force the conversion turns on is rarely named.** One title names a pitching moment, for an annular lift fan aircraft, and none names a normal force, although tunnel reports in the incidence cluster measure the loads on a duct at angle of attack, of which those are the components. The estimate of that force above therefore rests on the momentum relation and on the full-scale duct test.

**The shroud's thrust gain is measured but not named.** Three titles name static thrust together with a duct or shroud, and none uses the phrase thrust augmentation, so the twenty-six percent rests on the momentum relation derived above.

**The cross-shaft has almost no literature of its own.** One title names a shaft-coupled drive, a 1977 analysis of control concepts for gas-coupled and shaft-coupled lift fan systems, and the 11 records of the transmission cluster concern drive trains, gearboxes and shafts rather than the interconnection of separate lift units.

**Duct buzz has none.** No title in the survey names buzz, and the account of it above rests on the initial Navy flight tests as summarised in NASA CR-1901.

**Height damping is a helicopter subject in the literature.** Four titles name height control or a vertical rate command. Three concern helicopters or rotorcraft, and the fourth is a 1964 piloted motion simulator study of height-control requirements for vertical-takeoff aircraft. The low natural height damping that the Calspan pilots met in the X-22A is documented in the programme's own reports rather than in a literature of its own.

**The exit vane appears twice.** Two titles name vanes in a duct, a 1959 study of a ducted fan with turning vanes and a 2021 computation of a ducted fan and vane configuration in hover, and no title names an elevon.

**Engine-out hover is a recent subject.** Seven titles name an engine failure or an inoperative engine together with a vertical-flight vehicle, all of them published in 2019 or later, and none names a cross-shafted or interconnected drive, which is the arrangement that makes the X-22A's engine-out case a loss of power rather than of symmetry.

### What the Survey Admits

**A record is admitted only when a person reading its title finds it on this article's subject**, which is the ducted propeller, the vertical-takeoff problem around it, and the handling-qualities work the X-22 served. Many words this subject depends on also name something else, and a record that shares only such a word is excluded.

**A ducted propeller is a marine technology, an aeronautical one and a wind-energy one**, and a title search on the phrase returns all three. The Kort nozzle is a duct around a ship's propeller and its literature is large, old and entirely irrelevant here. Diffuser-augmented wind turbines and tidal turbines are ducted rotors whose momentum theory is the same and whose application is not. **Marine, tidal and wind-turbine records are excluded from the reference base**, and often only the venue separates them, because their titles are indistinguishable from the aeronautical ones.

Shared words also mislead in the other direction. A paper on the United States **Marine** Corps and vertical takeoff is not marine engineering and belongs here, and a paper on **Building** Credible Flight Models is not building aerodynamics. The phrase **tandem propeller**, by contrast, also describes a submarine.

**Five more words are shared, and four of the five are shared inside engineering rather than outside it.** Trim means the aircraft's balance, the interior fittings, the engine setting and the attitude of a planing hull, so the word alone gathers door seals and engine test sets. **Inlet** belongs to propulsion and to coastal geomorphology, where a tidal inlet is the channel between barrier islands, so inlet flow is also a subject in the literature of sediment transport. **Figure of merit** is the hover efficiency of a rotor and also a performance metric in thermoelectric materials, electronic devices, optics and nuclear-radiation detectors. **Electric propulsion** means an aircraft motor here and an ion thruster in the spacecraft literature, where it covers subjects such as optimal low-thrust interplanetary trajectories. And **transition** is the conversion from hover to wingborne flight here and the change from a laminar to a turbulent boundary layer in aerodynamics generally, so a study of transition on a hovering rotor blade belongs to hover performance and a study of transition on a slender re-entry body does not belong at all. The ducted-fan engine of the 1940s and 1950s is the turbofan, and ducted propagation is a phenomenon of radio and ocean acoustics.

Excluded besides the homonym families are community noise studies that contain no aircraft, ducts that carry steam, radiation or fuel sprays, the disc loading of hummingbird wings and the aerodynamics of flapping wings, wing-in-ground-effect craft, supersonic and swept-wing aerodynamics and wave drag, jet mixing, pulsejets, ducted rockets and rocket nozzles in ducts, the handling qualities of road vehicles, room and duct silencing acoustics, signal-processing noise reduction, machines that use a ducted fan only as an actuator, such as a crane, a pipeline robot and a wall-climbing robot, and records that are not works, meaning figures, tables, supplementary files, peer-review reports, notices of erratum, correction, retraction or withdrawal, and journal front matter. A review of a book, a dictionary or short encyclopedia entry and a notice such as an editorial, an announcement or a news item are not research works either and are excluded. A document deposited twice under two identifiers is counted once. Doubtful records are kept where the problem is shared, such as the downwash load of an agricultural spraying aircraft, a cross-Channel hovercraft and an exterior noise test facility, because each bears on a consequence of high disc loading that the X-22 also produced. Engine nacelle inlets in crosswind are kept because their lip separates for the reason a duct lip does.

**Every research title has been read for relevance, so the off-topic share that remains is a matter of reading judgement.** A reading of titles cannot show that a work is relevant in substance, so this is a statement about titles only. Three further doubtful classes are kept besides those just named. The aeroacoustics of axial fans in ducts outside aircraft, such as ventilation and server-rack fans, is kept because the fan and duct physics is the same. The handling qualities of spacecraft and lunar landers are kept because their criteria were developed by the same method as the vertical-takeoff criteria. Noise studies of aircraft in general, such as airport and landing-gear noise, are kept because they frame the community-noise problem that ducted fans are now asked to meet.

## Epistemic State

**Established and not in dispute.** The aircraft first flew in 1966, one of two was lost in 1966, and the survivor operated as a variable-stability in-flight simulator from 1968 and was still flying research programmes in 1980 on a 500-hour service life.

**Documented in primary reports.** The full-scale duct's area ratio of 1.165, figure of merit of 81 percent on exit area, propulsive efficiency of 74 percent and lip stall boundaries. The thrust-to-weight ratio of 1.35. The variable-stability system's architecture and limits, and the results of the four research programmes completed by 1979.

**Disputed among secondary accounts.** The cause of the 1966 accident, the first flight of the second aircraft, the last flight in 1984 or 1988, the career hours, and whether the specified maximum speed was reached.

**Computed here from quoted figures.** The 26 percent thrust augmentation follows from momentum theory given the exit area ratio. The figure of merit of 0.716 follows from reading the quoted 35 percent as excess power, and reproduces the assumed three-engine hover weight to 0.83 percent. The documented reading, a thrust-to-weight ratio of 1.35, leaves an eleven percent thrust margin on three engines, and the tunnel's best figure of merit makes it reachable at the 15,000 pound design weight only with no installation loss.

**Not supported.** That the 35 percent was chosen as the bare minimum for three-engine hover. That reading requires the figure to be excess power, and the primary statement gives it as thrust to weight.

**Weakly supported.** The magnitude of the duct normal force, which depends on an assumed lip factor, and the position of its peak, which falls later in the conversion than the measured pitching-moment peak.

**Not addressed.** A model of the tandem interference, and the structural and weight cost of the cross-shafted transmission.

## Out of Scope

The detailed design of the transmission. The variable-stability system's own control laws, which are a subject in their own right. The tri-service requirement that produced the programme and the politics of its cancellation. Comparison with the tilt-rotor line that eventually succeeded, which belongs to a later article.

## Conclusion

**The duct is the aeroplane.** It buys twenty-six percent more static thrust for nothing but a ring of metal, which is what makes a seven-foot propeller carry ninety-five pounds per square foot, and it charges for that in the conversion by acting as an annular wing whose normal force peaks at about its own thrust in the middle of the manoeuvre.

**The margin depends on what the 35 percent measured.** Read as excess power it leaves one and a quarter percent after an engine fails, and read as thrust to weight, which is how the NASA survey states it, it leaves eleven percent. The full-scale duct's measured figure of merit makes the thrust reading at the 15,000 pound design weight possible only with almost no installation loss, so the design was at best tight, and the record read here does not settle how tight.

**And the aeroplane outlived its own question.** It was built to find out whether four ducted propellers made a practical transport, and the answer was that they made a practical aircraft and an unattractive transport. What kept it flying for eighteen years was that somebody realised a machine which can hover and convert, and whose control system can be reprogrammed to feel like something else, is more valuable as a laboratory than as a prototype.

**No other aircraft in this series was rescued that way.** The X-18 was scrapped, the X-19 crashed, the X-20 was cancelled and the X-21 was retired to a photographic target. The X-22 was repurposed, and it is the only one of them still whole, stored at the Niagara Aerospace Museum according to the [Wikipedia entry][ref_x22].
